What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because... honestly, it’s about flying. Getting on an airplane now scares me so badly that I know, logically, it doesn’t really make sense. I can tell myself all the reasonable things, you know, that planes fly every day and people are fine, but my body just doesn’t believe it. It’s like the moment I think about actually boarding one, there’s this certainty in me that the plane is going to fall. Like not just a worry, but my body reacts as if it’s going to happen. And it’s gotten bad enough that I felt like I needed to talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Is it just flying that triggers this kind of fear and physical reaction, or are there other situations that cause you to feel similarly?\n\nPatient: It’s really the flying. Or, more specifically, the thought of actually getting on the airplane. I mean, I can talk about planes in an abstract way and I understand, rationally, that people fly all the time, but when it becomes me having to board, my whole mind just locks onto this idea that the plane is going to fall.\n\nAnd I know that sounds irrational. I’m not sitting here saying I have evidence for it. It’s just… that’s what makes it so frustrating. My brain can say one thing, but my body reacts like it’s a certainty, like once I’m on there, something terrible is going to happen and the plane won’t stay up. That’s the fear that brought me here.\n\nDoctor: Do you always experience this fear when you think about flying, or are there times when you can think about it without feeling scared?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s pretty much immediate if it feels connected to me actually having to fly. It’s not just when I’m at the airport or something dramatic like that. Even if I only get an email about a rural shift that would require a flight, before I’ve even fully read it, my heart starts pounding. My hands get sweaty right away, and I get this awful feeling like I can’t breathe properly.\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds excessive because it’s just an email, right? I’m not even on a plane. I’m sitting there looking at my inbox. But as soon as my brain registers, “This means you might have to get on a plane,” it’s like my body reacts instantly. I usually feel like I have to close the message just to get air again, if that makes sense. So, no, I wouldn’t say there are many times I can think about it in a real way without getting scared. If it’s hypothetical and distant maybe it’s a little easier, but the moment it feels like it could actually involve me flying, the fear kicks in right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've ever had what you would describe as a panic attack, where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… at least, it feels like that to me. It can come on really fast. Like I mentioned, even before I’m anywhere near an airport, if I just get an email about a rural shift that would mean I need to fly, my body reacts almost immediately.\n\nMy heart starts pounding right away, my hands get sweaty, and then I get this feeling like I can’t breathe properly. It’s embarrassing because I’m literally just sitting there looking at a message, but in that moment it feels like the fear spikes so quickly that I have to close the email just to calm down enough to breathe again. So, yes, if that counts as a panic attack, then I think I have had that kind of experience.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear exclusively about the plane crashing, or are there other aspects of flying that scare you?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly the plane falling, honestly. I don’t mean things like the seats being cramped or the airport being stressful or being around strangers. I mean, those things aren’t pleasant, but they’re not what brought me here.\n\nThe thing that gets me is the thought of actually getting on the airplane and then my mind just goes straight to, “This plane is going to fall.” And I know, sitting here, that doesn’t make logical sense. I can say that out loud. I know planes fly every day. But when it’s me having to board one, it’s like my body doesn’t believe any of that. My body reacts as if the plane is definitely not going to stay up, as if something awful is going to happen once I’m on it.\n\nSo yes, I’d say the core fear is the crash, or the plane coming down. That’s really why I came in, because the thought of getting on an airplane scares me so badly now that even though part of me knows it’s not rational, I can’t seem to make my body understand that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of flying something that you experience every single time you think about getting on a plane?\n\nPatient: Yes… if I’m thinking about it as something I actually have to do, then yes, it’s every time. It’s not like I can sit there and calmly picture myself getting on a plane and just feel neutral about it.\n\nEven something small sets it off. Like if I get an email about a rural shift and I realize it would involve flying, my body reacts before I can even reason with myself. My heart starts pounding right away, my hands get sweaty, and I get this tight feeling like I can’t breathe properly. And I’ll end up closing the message because it feels like I can’t settle until I stop looking at it.\n\nSo, yes, it’s basically every time the thought becomes real — like, “I might have to get on a plane.” That’s when it hits immediately.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of flying affected your work, social life, or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes. Quite a lot, actually. It’s not just that I feel scared and then life goes on normally. It’s started to affect practical things.\n\nWith work, the biggest issue is coverage that would require flying. I’ve turned down shifts or coverage opportunities because they were in rural places I’d need to fly to, and that has cost me income. And I hate saying that because it sounds like I’m just being difficult or picky, but I’m not. I’ll look at the opportunity and think, “This would be good financially, this is work I could do,” and then as soon as I realize flying is involved, I back out.\n\nIt has also affected how people see me at work, I think. Some colleagues have started to question whether they can really rely on me for certain coverage, especially if there’s travel involved. I don’t think everyone says it directly, but I can feel it. Or sometimes it’s implied, like they stop asking me for certain things. That bothers me a lot, because professionally I don’t want to be seen as unreliable.\n\nI’ve also missed important professional meetings because I couldn’t get myself to fly. Some of those would have been useful for my career, networking, keeping up with things, just being present. And outside of work, I’ve delayed seeing my family because traveling to them would involve getting on a plane. That part makes me feel pretty guilty, honestly, because it’s not that I don’t want to see them. I do. I just keep postponing it because of the flight.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of flying the only thing that you're afraid of, or are there other things that scare you in a similar way?\n\nPatient: It’s really the flying. Or, more specifically, getting on the airplane itself and feeling like that plane is not going to stay up.\n\nI mean, I get nervous about normal things like anyone does, but nothing else has this same quality where my mind and body just take over. That’s why I came in. The thought of getting on an airplane now scares me so badly that even though I know, rationally, it doesn’t make sense, my body is telling me, “That plane is going to fall.” It feels so certain in the moment, even if I can sit here right now and say I know that isn’t logical.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say there are a bunch of other fears like this. This is the one that feels out of proportion and hard to control.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of flying?\n\nPatient: It’s been about seven months now. It started after this small-plane flight I had to take after I lost my clinic job.\n\nI don’t think I was always like this with flying, at least not to this degree. That flight was really turbulent, and ever since then it’s like something shifted. I can sort of mark time from that point, because before that I might have been uncomfortable or not thrilled about flying, but after that flight it became… this thing I can’t get around.\n\nSo, yes, roughly seven months. It feels longer, honestly, because it’s affected so many decisions since then, but if I’m being accurate, that’s when it started.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, beyond work and seeing your family, where your fear of flying has caused you significant distress or problems?\n\nPatient: I think the main areas really are work and family, but they bleed into other parts of life too. It’s hard to separate them neatly.\n\nLike, turning down flight-based coverage hasn’t just been a scheduling thing. It has cost me income, and then I start thinking about what that means longer term — whether I’m limiting myself, whether I’m becoming someone people don’t ask anymore because they assume I won’t go if there’s a plane involved. I’ve noticed colleagues questioning, maybe not always directly, whether I can be relied on for certain assignments, and that’s been really distressing for me.\n\nAnd then the professional meetings I’ve missed — those bother me beyond just missing a trip. It feels like I’m absent from parts of my own career, if that makes sense. Opportunities to be seen, to keep up, to connect with people, and I’m not there because I can’t get myself on the flight.\n\nFamily is the other big one. I’ve delayed visits, and even when nobody is angry with me, I feel guilty. I keep having to explain or make excuses, and I don’t like what that’s doing to those relationships.\n\nSo beyond those, I guess it’s more the overall stress and the way it’s narrowing my life. But the concrete problems are really income, reliability at work, missed professional meetings, and putting off seeing my family.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear of flying is the main thing that's causing you distress, or are there other worries or anxieties that also contribute to how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly the flying. I mean, I have ordinary worries, of course, but the reason I came here is because this airplane fear has gotten so intense that it feels like it’s running the show.\n\nWhen I think about actually getting on a plane, not just talking about it in the abstract but walking down the jet bridge or sitting there waiting for takeoff, it scares me so badly that even though part of me knows it doesn’t make sense, my body reacts like the danger is completely real. It’s like everything in me is saying, that plane is going to fall, and I can’t talk myself out of it in the moment.\n\nSo yes, I’d say that’s the main thing causing the distress. Not some general cloud of anxiety about everything. It’s this specific situation, this fear of being on the airplane and believing it won’t stay up, even when I know rationally that sounds unreasonable.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time, since this fear started, where you didn't feel afraid of flying?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, since that flight, it’s been pretty continuous. There may be moments where I’m not thinking about flying because it’s not right in front of me, but if the idea of actually getting on a plane comes up, the fear is still there.\n\nIt’s been about seven months, since that turbulent small-plane flight after I lost my clinic job, and I can’t think of a stretch during that time where I felt normal about flying again. It hasn’t really let up in any meaningful way.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities or hobbies that you've had to change or avoid because of your fear of flying?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, not hobbies in the sense of, like, I stopped playing a sport or something, but day-to-day life has changed around anything that could involve flying.\n\nI’ve canceled clinic coverage because I knew I would have to fly for it, and I just couldn’t make myself do it. And seeing my family in Honduras has been put off again and again, which is probably the part that makes me feel the worst.\n\nEven going to the airport itself is not something I can do normally anymore. If I absolutely have to be there, I’m shaking, and I feel like I need to hold on to someone the whole time just to get through it. So I arrange my life to avoid getting to that point. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I know how it sounds, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other fears or anxieties that are as intense as your fear of flying?\n\nPatient: No, not like that. My hands get sweaty right away, and I get this awful feeling like I can’t breathe properly.\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds excessive because it’s just an email, right? I’m not even on a plane. I’m sitting there looking at my inbox. But as soon as my brain registers, “This means you might have to get on a plane,” it’s like my body reacts instantly. I usually feel like I have to close the message just to get air again, if that makes sense. So, no, I wouldn’t say there are many times I can think about it in a real way without getting scared. If it’s hypothetical and distant maybe it’s a little easier, but the moment it feels like it could actually involve me flying, the fear kicks in right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've ever had what you would describe as a panic attack, where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… at least, it feels like that to me. It can come on really fast. Like I mentioned, even before I’m anywhere near an airport, if I just get an email about a rural shift that would mean I need to fly, my body reacts almost immediately.\n\nMy heart starts pounding right away, my hands get sweaty, and then I get this feeling like I can’t breathe properly. It’s embarrassing because I’m literally just sitting there looking at a message, but in that moment it feels like the fear spikes so quickly that I have to close the email just to calm down enough to breathe again. So, yes, if that counts as a panic attack, then I think I have had that kind of experience.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear exclusively about the plane crashing, or are there other aspects of flying that scare you?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly the plane falling, honestly. I don’t mean things like the seats being cramped or the airport being stressful or being around strangers. I mean, those things aren’t pleasant, but they’re not what brought me here.\n\nThe thing that gets me is the thought of actually getting on the airplane and then my mind just goes straight to, “This plane is going to fall.” And I know, sitting here, that doesn’t make logical sense. I can say that out loud. I know planes fly every day. But when it’s me having to board one, it’s like my body doesn’t believe any of that. My body reacts as if the plane is definitely not going to stay up, as if something awful is going to happen once I’m on it.\n\nSo yes, I’d say the core fear is the crash, or the plane coming down. That’s really why I came in, because the thought of getting on an airplane scares me so badly now that even though part of me knows it’s not rational, I can’t seem to make my body understand that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of flying something that you experience every single time you think about getting on a plane?\n\nPatient: Yes… if I’m thinking about it as something I actually have to do, then yes, it’s every time. It’s not like I can sit there and calmly picture myself getting on a plane and just feel neutral about it.\n\nEven something small sets it off. Like if I get an email about a rural shift and I realize it would involve flying, my body reacts before I can even reason with myself. My heart starts pounding right away, my hands get sweaty, and I get this tight feeling like I can’t breathe properly. And I’ll end up closing the message because it feels like I can’t settle until I stop looking at it.\n\nSo, yes, it’s basically every time the thought becomes real — like, “I might have to get on a plane.” That’s when it hits immediately.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of flying affected your work, social life, or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes. Quite a lot, actually. It’s not just that I feel scared and then life goes on normally. It’s started to affect practical things.\n\nWith work, the biggest issue is coverage that would require flying. I’ve turned down shifts or coverage opportunities because they were in rural places I’d need to fly to, and that has cost me income. And I hate saying that because it sounds like I’m just being difficult or picky, but I’m not. I’ll look at the opportunity and think, “This would be good financially, this is work I could do,” and then as soon as I realize flying is involved, I back out.\n\nIt has also affected how people see me at work, I think. Some colleagues have started to question whether they can really rely on me for certain coverage, especially if there’s travel involved. I don’t think everyone says it directly, but I can feel it. Or sometimes it’s implied, like they stop asking me for certain things. That bothers me a lot, because professionally I don’t want to be seen as unreliable.\n\nI’ve also missed important professional meetings because I couldn’t get myself to fly. Some of those would have been useful for my career, networking, keeping up with things, just being present. And outside of work, I’ve delayed seeing my family because traveling to them would involve getting on a plane. That part makes me feel pretty guilty, honestly, because it’s not that I don’t want to see them. I do. I just keep postponing it because of the flight.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of flying the only thing that you're afraid of, or are there other things that scare you in a similar way?\n\nPatient: It’s really the flying. Or, more specifically, getting on the airplane itself and feeling like that plane is not going to stay up.\n\nI mean, I get nervous about normal things like anyone does, but nothing else has this same quality where my mind and body just take over. That’s why I came in. The thought of getting on an airplane now scares me so badly that even though I know, rationally, it doesn’t make sense, my body is telling me, “That plane is going to fall.” It feels so certain in the moment, even if I can sit here right now and say I know that isn’t logical.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say there are a bunch of other fears like this. This is the one that feels out of proportion and hard to control.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of flying?\n\nPatient: It’s been about seven months now. It started after this small-plane flight I had to take after I lost my clinic job.\n\nI don’t think I was always like this with flying, at least not to this degree. That flight was really turbulent, and ever since then it’s like something shifted. I can sort of mark time from that point, because before that I might have been uncomfortable or not thrilled about flying, but after that flight it became… this thing I can’t get around.\n\nSo, yes, roughly seven months. It feels longer, honestly, because it’s affected so many decisions since then, but if I’m being accurate, that’s when it started.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, beyond work and seeing your family, where your fear of flying has caused you significant distress or problems?\n\nPatient: I think the main areas really are work and family, but they bleed into other parts of life too. It’s hard to separate them neatly.\n\nLike, turning down flight-based coverage hasn’t just been a scheduling thing. It has cost me income, and then I start thinking about what that means longer term — whether I’m limiting myself, whether I’m becoming someone people don’t ask anymore because they assume I won’t go if there’s a plane involved. I’ve noticed colleagues questioning, maybe not always directly, whether I can be relied on for certain assignments, and that’s been really distressing for me.\n\nAnd then the professional meetings I’ve missed — those bother me beyond just missing a trip. It feels like I’m absent from parts of my own career, if that makes sense. Opportunities to be seen, to keep up, to connect with people, and I’m not there because I can’t get myself on the flight.\n\nFamily is the other big one. I’ve delayed visits, and even when nobody is angry with me, I feel guilty. I keep having to explain or make excuses, and I don’t like what that’s doing to those relationships.\n\nSo beyond those, I guess it’s more the overall stress and the way it’s narrowing my life. But the concrete problems are really income, reliability at work, missed professional meetings, and putting off seeing my family.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear of flying is the main thing that's causing you distress, or are there other worries or anxieties that also contribute to how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly the flying. I mean, I have ordinary worries, of course, but the reason I came here is because this airplane fear has gotten so intense that it feels like it’s running the show.\n\nWhen I think about actually getting on a plane, not just talking about it in the abstract but walking down the jet bridge or sitting there waiting for takeoff, it scares me so badly that even though part of me knows it doesn’t make sense, my body reacts like the danger is completely real. It’s like everything in me is saying, that plane is going to fall, and I can’t talk myself out of it in the moment.\n\nSo yes, I’d say that’s the main thing causing the distress. Not some general cloud of anxiety about everything. It’s this specific situation, this fear of being on the airplane and believing it won’t stay up, even when I know rationally that sounds unreasonable.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time, since this fear started, where you didn't feel afraid of flying?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, since that flight, it’s been pretty continuous. There may be moments where I’m not thinking about flying because it’s not right in front of me, but if the idea of actually getting on a plane comes up, the fear is still there.\n\nIt’s been about seven months, since that turbulent small-plane flight after I lost my clinic job, and I can’t think of a stretch during that time where I felt normal about flying again. It hasn’t really let up in any meaningful way.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities or hobbies that you've had to change or avoid because of your fear of flying?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, not hobbies in the sense of, like, I stopped playing a sport or something, but day-to-day life has changed around anything that could involve flying.\n\nI’ve canceled clinic coverage because I knew I would have to fly for it, and I just couldn’t make myself do it. I’ve skipped medical meetings too, even ones I knew I should attend, because getting to the airport felt impossible. And seeing my family in Honduras has been put off again and again, which is probably the part that makes me feel the worst.\n\nEven going to the airport itself is not something I can do normally anymore. If I absolutely have to be there, I’m shaking, and I feel like I need to hold on to someone the whole time just to get through it. So I arrange my life to avoid getting to that point. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I know how it sounds, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other fears or anxieties that are as intense as your fear of flying?\n\nPatient: No, not like that. I mean, I worry about things, like anyone does, but nothing hits me the way flying does now.\n\nThat’s really why I came here. The thought of getting on an airplane scares me so badly that even as I’m telling myself, “This doesn’t make sense, planes fly every day,” my body doesn’t believe it. My body reacts like it knows for certain that the plane is going to fall.\n\nIt’s not that I have that same level of fear with other situations. This is the one that feels out of proportion, like I can understand it logically but I can’t make myself feel safe.\n\nDoctor: Are there any aspects of your daily routine or personal relationships that you feel are being significantly impacted by your fear of flying?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. It’s not just an inconvenience anymore. It’s less about a time of year, I think, and more about anything that suddenly makes the flight feel real. Like even if I only get an email about a rural shift that would require flying, my body reacts immediately.

I’ll be sitting there doing something normal, and then I see the message, and right away my heart starts pounding. I haven’t booked anything, I’m not at an airport, I’m not on a plane — it’s just an email. But my body reacts like I’m already trapped in it.

So I don’t know that I’ve noticed a seasonal pattern or anything like that. The trigger is more the possibility of having to fly, especially when it’s connected to work and I feel like I’m going to have to decide or respond.

I don’t mean things like the seats being cramped or the airport being stressful or being around strangers. I mean, those things aren’t pleasant, but they’re not what brought me here.\n\nThe thing that gets me is the thought of actually getting on the airplane and then my mind just goes straight to, “This plane is going to fall.” And I know, sitting here, that doesn’t make logical sense. I can say that out loud. I know planes fly every day. But when it’s me having to board one, it’s like my body doesn’t believe any of that. My body reacts as if the plane is definitely not going to stay up, as if something awful is going to happen once I’m on it.\n\nSo yes, I’d say the core fear is the crash, or the plane coming down. That’s really why I came in, because the thought of getting on an airplane scares me so badly now that even though part of me knows it’s not rational, I can’t seem to make my body understand that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of flying something that you experience every single time you think about getting on a plane?\n\nPatient: Yes… if I’m thinking about it as something I actually have to do, then yes, it’s every time. It’s not like I can sit there and calmly picture myself getting on a plane and just feel neutral about it.\n\nEven something small sets it off. Like if I get an email about a rural shift and I realize it would involve flying, my body reacts before I can even reason with myself. My heart starts pounding right away, my hands get sweaty, and I get this tight feeling like I can’t breathe properly. And I’ll end up closing the message because it feels like I can’t settle until I stop looking at it.\n\nSo, yes, it’s basically every time the thought becomes real — like, “I might have to get on a plane.” That’s when it hits immediately.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of flying affected your work, social life, or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes. Quite a lot, actually. It’s not just that I feel scared and then life goes on normally. It’s started to affect practical things.\n\nWith work, the biggest issue is coverage that would require flying. I’ve turned down shifts or coverage opportunities because they were in rural places I’d need to fly to, and that has cost me income. And I hate saying that because it sounds like I’m just being difficult or picky, but I’m not. I’ll look at the opportunity and think, “This would be good financially, this is work I could do,” and then as soon as I realize flying is involved, I back out.\n\nIt has also affected how people see me at work, I think. Some colleagues have started to question whether they can really rely on me for certain coverage, especially if there’s travel involved. I don’t think everyone says it directly, but I can feel it. Or sometimes it’s implied, like they stop asking me for certain things. That bothers me a lot, because professionally I don’t want to be seen as unreliable.\n\nI’ve also missed important professional meetings because I couldn’t get myself to fly. Some of those would have been useful for my career, networking, keeping up with things, just being present. And outside of work, I’ve delayed seeing my family because traveling to them would involve getting on a plane. That part makes me feel pretty guilty, honestly, because it’s not that I don’t want to see them. I do. I just keep postponing it because of the flight.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of flying the only thing that you're afraid of, or are there other things that scare you in a similar way?\n\nPatient: It’s really the flying. Or, more specifically, getting on the airplane itself and feeling like that plane is not going to stay up.\n\nI mean, I get nervous about normal things like anyone does, but nothing else has this same quality where my mind and body just take over. That’s why I came in. The thought of getting on an airplane now scares me so badly that even though I know, rationally, it doesn’t make sense, my body is telling me, “That plane is going to fall.” It feels so certain in the moment, even if I can sit here right now and say I know that isn’t logical.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say there are a bunch of other fears like this. This is the one that feels out of proportion and hard to control.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of flying?\n\nPatient: It’s been about seven months now. It started after this small-plane flight I had to take after I lost my clinic job.\n\nI don’t think I was always like this with flying, at least not to this degree. That flight was really turbulent, and ever since then it’s like something shifted. I can sort of mark time from that point, because before that I might have been uncomfortable or not thrilled about flying, but after that flight it became… this thing I can’t get around.\n\nSo, yes, roughly seven months. It feels longer, honestly, because it’s affected so many decisions since then, but if I’m being accurate, that’s when it started.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, beyond work and seeing your family, where your fear of flying has caused you significant distress or problems?\n\nPatient: I think the main areas really are work and family, but they bleed into other parts of life too. It’s hard to separate them neatly.\n\nLike, turning down flight-based coverage hasn’t just been a scheduling thing. It has cost me income, and then I start thinking about what that means longer term — whether I’m limiting myself, whether I’m becoming someone people don’t ask anymore because they assume I won’t go if there’s a plane involved. I’ve noticed colleagues questioning, maybe not always directly, whether I can be relied on for certain assignments, and that’s been really distressing for me.\n\nAnd then the professional meetings I’ve missed — those bother me beyond just missing a trip. It feels like I’m absent from parts of my own career, if that makes sense. Opportunities to be seen, to keep up, to connect with people, and I’m not there because I can’t get myself on the flight.\n\nFamily is the other big one. I’ve delayed visits, and even when nobody is angry with me, I feel guilty. I keep having to explain or make excuses, and I don’t like what that’s doing to those relationships.\n\nSo beyond those, I guess it’s more the overall stress and the way it’s narrowing my life. But the concrete problems are really income, reliability at work, missed professional meetings, and putting off seeing my family.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear of flying is the main thing that's causing you distress, or are there other worries or anxieties that also contribute to how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly the flying. I mean, I have ordinary worries, of course, but the reason I came here is because this airplane fear has gotten so intense that it feels like it’s running the show.\n\nWhen I think about actually getting on a plane, not just talking about it in the abstract but walking down the jet bridge or sitting there waiting for takeoff, it scares me so badly that even though part of me knows it doesn’t make sense, my body reacts like the danger is completely real. It’s like everything in me is saying, that plane is going to fall, and I can’t talk myself out of it in the moment.\n\nSo yes, I’d say that’s the main thing causing the distress. Not some general cloud of anxiety about everything. It’s this specific situation, this fear of being on the airplane and believing it won’t stay up, even when I know rationally that sounds unreasonable.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time, since this fear started, where you didn't feel afraid of flying?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, since that flight, it’s been pretty continuous. There may be moments where I’m not thinking about flying because it’s not right in front of me, but if the idea of actually getting on a plane comes up, the fear is still there.\n\nIt’s been about seven months, since that turbulent small-plane flight after I lost my clinic job, and I can’t think of a stretch during that time where I felt normal about flying again. It hasn’t really let up in any meaningful way.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities or hobbies that you've had to change or avoid because of your fear of flying?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, not hobbies in the sense of, like, I stopped playing a sport or something, but day-to-day life has changed around anything that could involve flying.\n\nI’ve canceled clinic coverage because I knew I would have to fly for it, and I just couldn’t make myself do it. I’ve skipped medical meetings too, even ones I knew I should attend, because getting to the airport felt impossible. And seeing my family in Honduras has been put off again and again, which is probably the part that makes me feel the worst.\n\nEven going to the airport itself is not something I can do normally anymore. If I absolutely have to be there, I’m shaking, and I feel like I need to hold on to someone the whole time just to get through it. So I arrange my life to avoid getting to that point. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I know how it sounds, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other fears or anxieties that are as intense as your fear of flying?\n\nPatient: No, not like that. I mean, I worry about things, like anyone does, but nothing hits me the way flying does now.\n\nThat’s really why I came here. The thought of getting on an airplane scares me so badly that even as I’m telling myself, “This doesn’t make sense, planes fly every day,” my body doesn’t believe it. My body reacts like it knows for certain that the plane is going to fall.\n\nIt’s not that I have that same level of fear with other situations. This is the one that feels out of proportion, like I can understand it logically but I can’t make myself feel safe.\n\nDoctor: Are there any aspects of your daily routine or personal relationships that you feel are being significantly impacted by your fear of flying?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. It’s not just an inconvenience anymore. It’s affecting work and family in ways I’m ashamed of.\n\nFor work, I’ve turned down coverage opportunities because they involved flying, and that has cost me income. But honestly, the money is only part of it. I worry that my colleagues are starting to see me as unreliable, like they can’t count on me when something requires travel. I’ve also missed professional meetings that would have mattered for my career, and I know people notice when you keep finding reasons not to go.\n\nAnd personally, it’s been hard because I’ve delayed seeing my family. I keep telling myself I’ll figure it out or that I’ll go later, but later keeps getting pushed back. That makes me feel guilty, because this fear is making decisions for me. It’s interfering with my life in a way I don’t feel able to explain very well to people without sounding like I’m making excuses.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar fear when thinking about traveling by other means, such as boats, trains, or buses?\n\nPatient: No, not in the same way. I mean, I don’t love every form of travel, but boats or trains or buses don’t make my body react like that.\n\nWith flying, it’s different. Just thinking about getting on an airplane now scares me so badly that even though I know, logically, it doesn’t make sense, my body is telling me the plane is going to fall. It’s like I can argue with myself in my head, but it doesn’t change the fear. That’s really why I came in, because it’s not just a preference or discomfort anymore. It feels completely out of proportion, and I can’t talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any patterns or triggers that make your fear of flying worse, such as specific times of the year or certain types of flights?\n\nPatient: It’s less about a time of year, I think, and more about anything that suddenly makes the flight feel real. Like even if I only get an email about a rural shift that would require flying, my body reacts immediately.\n\nI’ll be sitting there doing something normal, and then I see the message, and right away my heart starts pounding. My hands get sweaty, and I get this feeling like I can’t breathe properly until I close the email. It’s embarrassing because nothing has even happened yet. I haven’t booked anything, I’m not at an airport, I’m not on a plane — it’s just an email. But my body reacts like I’m already trapped in it.\n\nSo I don’t know that I’ve noticed a seasonal pattern or anything like that. The trigger is more the possibility of having to fly, especially when it’s connected to work and I feel like I’m going to have to decide or respond.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

No, not really. I mean, I’ve tried to think about that because I don’t want to make it sound more mysterious than it is, but it really seems centered on flying.

There are other things in life I dislike or avoid if I can, sure, but nothing hits me the way the idea of getting on a plane does. That’s why I came in, honestly. It feels like my body believes something terrible is guaranteed to happen, even when my mind is trying to argue against it.

So, no, I haven’t noticed that same intense fear in another specific situation. It’s the airplane part that seems to set it off.

I don’t mean things like the seats being cramped or the airport being stressful or being around strangers. I mean, those things aren’t pleasant, but they’re not what brought me here.\n\nThe thing that gets me is the thought of actually getting on the airplane and then my mind just goes straight to, “This plane is going to fall.” And I know, sitting here, that doesn’t make logical sense. I can say that out loud. I know planes fly every day. But when it’s me having to board one, it’s like my body doesn’t believe any of that. My body reacts as if the plane is definitely not going to stay up, as if something awful is going to happen once I’m on it.\n\nSo yes, I’d say the core fear is the crash, or the plane coming down. That’s really why I came in, because the thought of getting on an airplane scares me so badly now that even though part of me knows it’s not rational, I can’t seem to make my body understand that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of flying something that you experience every single time you think about getting on a plane?\n\nPatient: Yes… if I’m thinking about it as something I actually have to do, then yes, it’s every time. It’s not like I can sit there and calmly picture myself getting on a plane and just feel neutral about it.\n\nEven something small sets it off. Like if I get an email about a rural shift and I realize it would involve flying, my body reacts before I can even reason with myself. My heart starts pounding right away, my hands get sweaty, and I get this tight feeling like I can’t breathe properly. And I’ll end up closing the message because it feels like I can’t settle until I stop looking at it.\n\nSo, yes, it’s basically every time the thought becomes real — like, “I might have to get on a plane.” That’s when it hits immediately.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of flying affected your work, social life, or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes. Quite a lot, actually. It’s not just that I feel scared and then life goes on normally. It’s started to affect practical things.\n\nWith work, the biggest issue is coverage that would require flying. I’ve turned down shifts or coverage opportunities because they were in rural places I’d need to fly to, and that has cost me income. And I hate saying that because it sounds like I’m just being difficult or picky, but I’m not. I’ll look at the opportunity and think, “This would be good financially, this is work I could do,” and then as soon as I realize flying is involved, I back out.\n\nIt has also affected how people see me at work, I think. Some colleagues have started to question whether they can really rely on me for certain coverage, especially if there’s travel involved. I don’t think everyone says it directly, but I can feel it. Or sometimes it’s implied, like they stop asking me for certain things. That bothers me a lot, because professionally I don’t want to be seen as unreliable.\n\nI’ve also missed important professional meetings because I couldn’t get myself to fly. Some of those would have been useful for my career, networking, keeping up with things, just being present. And outside of work, I’ve delayed seeing my family because traveling to them would involve getting on a plane. That part makes me feel pretty guilty, honestly, because it’s not that I don’t want to see them. I do. I just keep postponing it because of the flight.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of flying the only thing that you're afraid of, or are there other things that scare you in a similar way?\n\nPatient: It’s really the flying. That’s why I came in. The thought of getting on an airplane now scares me so badly that even though I know, rationally, it doesn’t make sense, my body is telling me, “That plane is going to fall.” It feels so certain in the moment, even if I can sit here right now and say I know that isn’t logical.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say there are a bunch of other fears like this. This is the one that feels out of proportion and hard to control.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of flying?\n\nPatient: It’s been about seven months now. That flight was really turbulent, and ever since then it’s like something shifted. I’ve delayed visits, and even when nobody is angry with me, I feel guilty. I keep having to explain or make excuses, and I don’t like what that’s doing to those relationships.\n\nSo beyond those, I guess it’s more the overall stress and the way it’s narrowing my life. No, I don’t think so. I mean, I get stressed like anyone does, especially with work and responsibilities and all that, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had another fear that takes over my life the way this does.

I can tell myself all the reasonable things, you know, that planes fly every day and people are fine, but my body just doesn’t believe it. It’s like the moment I think about actually boarding one, there’s this certainty in me that the plane is going to fall. Like not just a worry, but my body reacts as if it’s going to happen. And it’s gotten bad enough that I felt like I needed to talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Is it just flying that triggers this kind of fear and physical reaction, or are there other situations that cause you to feel similarly?\n\nPatient: It’s really the flying. Or, more specifically, the thought of actually getting on the airplane. I mean, I can talk about planes in an abstract way and I understand, rationally, that people fly all the time, but when it becomes me having to board, my whole mind just locks onto this idea that the plane is going to fall.\n\nAnd I know that sounds irrational. I’m not sitting here saying I have evidence for it. It’s just… that’s what makes it so frustrating. My brain can say one thing, but my body reacts like it’s a certainty, like once I’m on there, something terrible is going to happen and the plane won’t stay up. That’s the fear that brought me here.\n\nDoctor: Do you always experience this fear when you think about flying, or are there times when you can think about it without feeling scared?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s pretty much immediate if it feels connected to me actually having to fly. It’s not just when I’m at the airport or something dramatic like that. Even if I only get an email about a rural shift that would require a flight, before I’ve even fully read it, my heart starts pounding. My hands get sweaty right away, and I get this awful feeling like I can’t breathe properly.\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds excessive because it’s just an email, right? I’m not even on a plane. I’m sitting there looking at my inbox. But as soon as my brain registers, “This means you might have to get on a plane,” it’s like my body reacts instantly. I usually feel like I have to close the message just to get air again, if that makes sense. So, no, I wouldn’t say there are many times I can think about it in a real way without getting scared. If it’s hypothetical and distant maybe it’s a little easier, but the moment it feels like it could actually involve me flying, the fear kicks in right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've ever had what you would describe as a panic attack, where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… at least, it feels like that to me. It can come on really fast. Like I mentioned, even before I’m anywhere near an airport, if I just get an email about a rural shift that would mean I need to fly, my body reacts almost immediately.\n\nMy heart starts pounding right away, my hands get sweaty, and then I get this feeling like I can’t breathe properly. It’s embarrassing because I’m literally just sitting there looking at a message, but in that moment it feels like the fear spikes so quickly that I have to close the email just to calm down enough to breathe again. So, yes, if that counts as a panic attack, then I think I have had that kind of experience.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear exclusively about the plane crashing, or are there other aspects of flying that scare you?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly the plane falling, honestly. I don’t mean things like the seats being cramped or the airport being stressful or being around strangers. I mean, those things aren’t pleasant, but they’re not what brought me here.\n\nThe thing that gets me is the thought of actually getting on the airplane and then my mind just goes straight to, “This plane is going to fall.” And I know, sitting here, that doesn’t make logical sense. I can say that out loud. I know planes fly every day. But when it’s me having to board one, it’s like my body doesn’t believe any of that. My body reacts as if the plane is definitely not going to stay up, as if something awful is going to happen once I’m on it.\n\nSo yes, I’d say the core fear is the crash, or the plane coming down. That’s really why I came in, because the thought of getting on an airplane scares me so badly now that even though part of me knows it’s not rational, I can’t seem to make my body understand that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of flying something that you experience every single time you think about getting on a plane?\n\nPatient: Yes… if I’m thinking about it as something I actually have to do, then yes, it’s every time. It’s not like I can sit there and calmly picture myself getting on a plane and just feel neutral about it.\n\nEven something small sets it off. Like if I get an email about a rural shift and I realize it would involve flying, my body reacts before I can even reason with myself. My heart starts pounding right away, my hands get sweaty, and I get this tight feeling like I can’t breathe properly. And I’ll end up closing the message because it feels like I can’t settle until I stop looking at it.\n\nSo, yes, it’s basically every time the thought becomes real — like, “I might have to get on a plane.” That’s when it hits immediately.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of flying affected your work, social life, or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes. Quite a lot, actually. It’s not just that I feel scared and then life goes on normally. It’s started to affect practical things.\n\nWith work, the biggest issue is coverage that would require flying. I’ve turned down shifts or coverage opportunities because they were in rural places I’d need to fly to, and that has cost me income. And I hate saying that because it sounds like I’m just being difficult or picky, but I’m not. I’ll look at the opportunity and think, “This would be good financially, this is work I could do,” and then as soon as I realize flying is involved, I back out.\n\nIt has also affected how people see me at work, I think. Some colleagues have started to question whether they can really rely on me for certain coverage, especially if there’s travel involved. I don’t think everyone says it directly, but I can feel it. Or sometimes it’s implied, like they stop asking me for certain things. That bothers me a lot, because professionally I don’t want to be seen as unreliable.\n\nI’ve also missed important professional meetings because I couldn’t get myself to fly. Some of those would have been useful for my career, networking, keeping up with things, just being present. And outside of work, I’ve delayed seeing my family because traveling to them would involve getting on a plane. That part makes me feel pretty guilty, honestly, because it’s not that I don’t want to see them. I do. I just keep postponing it because of the flight.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of flying the only thing that you're afraid of, or are there other things that scare you in a similar way?\n\nPatient: It’s really the flying. Or, more specifically, getting on the airplane itself and feeling like that plane is not going to stay up.\n\nI mean, I get nervous about normal things like anyone does, but nothing else has this same quality where my mind and body just take over. That’s why I came in. The thought of getting on an airplane now scares me so badly that even though I know, rationally, it doesn’t make sense, my body is telling me, “That plane is going to fall.” It feels so certain in the moment, even if I can sit here right now and say I know that isn’t logical.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say there are a bunch of other fears like this. This is the one that feels out of proportion and hard to control.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this fear of flying?\n\nPatient: It’s been about seven months now. It started after this small-plane flight I had to take after I lost my clinic job.\n\nI don’t think I was always like this with flying, at least not to this degree. That flight was really turbulent, and ever since then it’s like something shifted. I can sort of mark time from that point, because before that I might have been uncomfortable or not thrilled about flying, but after that flight it became… this thing I can’t get around.\n\nSo, yes, roughly seven months. It feels longer, honestly, because it’s affected so many decisions since then, but if I’m being accurate, that’s when it started.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, beyond work and seeing your family, where your fear of flying has caused you significant distress or problems?\n\nPatient: I think the main areas really are work and family, but they bleed into other parts of life too. It’s hard to separate them neatly.\n\nLike, turning down flight-based coverage hasn’t just been a scheduling thing. It has cost me income, and then I start thinking about what that means longer term — whether I’m limiting myself, whether I’m becoming someone people don’t ask anymore because they assume I won’t go if there’s a plane involved. I’ve noticed colleagues questioning, maybe not always directly, whether I can be relied on for certain assignments, and that’s been really distressing for me.\n\nAnd then the professional meetings I’ve missed — those bother me beyond just missing a trip. It feels like I’m absent from parts of my own career, if that makes sense. Opportunities to be seen, to keep up, to connect with people, and I’m not there because I can’t get myself on the flight.\n\nFamily is the other big one. I’ve delayed visits, and even when nobody is angry with me, I feel guilty. I keep having to explain or make excuses, and I don’t like what that’s doing to those relationships.\n\nSo beyond those, I guess it’s more the overall stress and the way it’s narrowing my life. But the concrete problems are really income, reliability at work, missed professional meetings, and putting off seeing my family.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear of flying is the main thing that's causing you distress, or are there other worries or anxieties that also contribute to how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly the flying. I mean, I have ordinary worries, of course, but the reason I came here is because this airplane fear has gotten so intense that it feels like it’s running the show.\n\nWhen I think about actually getting on a plane, not just talking about it in the abstract but walking down the jet bridge or sitting there waiting for takeoff, it scares me so badly that even though part of me knows it doesn’t make sense, my body reacts like the danger is completely real. It’s like everything in me is saying, that plane is going to fall, and I can’t talk myself out of it in the moment.\n\nSo yes, I’d say that’s the main thing causing the distress. Not some general cloud of anxiety about everything. It’s this specific situation, this fear of being on the airplane and believing it won’t stay up, even when I know rationally that sounds unreasonable.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time, since this fear started, where you didn't feel afraid of flying?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, since that flight, it’s been pretty continuous. There may be moments where I’m not thinking about flying because it’s not right in front of me, but if the idea of actually getting on a plane comes up, the fear is still there.\n\nIt’s been about seven months, since that turbulent small-plane flight after I lost my clinic job, and I can’t think of a stretch during that time where I felt normal about flying again. It hasn’t really let up in any meaningful way.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities or hobbies that you've had to change or avoid because of your fear of flying?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, not hobbies in the sense of, like, I stopped playing a sport or something, but day-to-day life has changed around anything that could involve flying.\n\nI’ve canceled clinic coverage because I knew I would have to fly for it, and I just couldn’t make myself do it. I’ve skipped medical meetings too, even ones I knew I should attend, because getting to the airport felt impossible. And seeing my family in Honduras has been put off again and again, which is probably the part that makes me feel the worst.\n\nEven going to the airport itself is not something I can do normally anymore. If I absolutely have to be there, I’m shaking, and I feel like I need to hold on to someone the whole time just to get through it. So I arrange my life to avoid getting to that point. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I know how it sounds, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other fears or anxieties that are as intense as your fear of flying?\n\nPatient: No, not like that. I mean, I worry about things, like anyone does, but nothing hits me the way flying does now.\n\nThat’s really why I came here. The thought of getting on an airplane scares me so badly that even as I’m telling myself, “This doesn’t make sense, planes fly every day,” my body doesn’t believe it. My body reacts like it knows for certain that the plane is going to fall.\n\nIt’s not that I have that same level of fear with other situations. This is the one that feels out of proportion, like I can understand it logically but I can’t make myself feel safe.\n\nDoctor: Are there any aspects of your daily routine or personal relationships that you feel are being significantly impacted by your fear of flying?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. It’s not just an inconvenience anymore. It’s affecting work and family in ways I’m ashamed of.\n\nFor work, I’ve turned down coverage opportunities because they involved flying, and that has cost me income. But honestly, the money is only part of it. I worry that my colleagues are starting to see me as unreliable, like they can’t count on me when something requires travel. I’ve also missed professional meetings that would have mattered for my career, and I know people notice when you keep finding reasons not to go.\n\nAnd personally, it’s been hard because I’ve delayed seeing my family. I keep telling myself I’ll figure it out or that I’ll go later, but later keeps getting pushed back. That makes me feel guilty, because this fear is making decisions for me. It’s interfering with my life in a way I don’t feel able to explain very well to people without sounding like I’m making excuses.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar fear when thinking about traveling by other means, such as boats, trains, or buses?\n\nPatient: No, not in the same way. I mean, I don’t love every form of travel, but boats or trains or buses don’t make my body react like that.\n\nWith flying, it’s different. Just thinking about getting on an airplane now scares me so badly that even though I know, logically, it doesn’t make sense, my body is telling me the plane is going to fall. It’s like I can argue with myself in my head, but it doesn’t change the fear. That’s really why I came in, because it’s not just a preference or discomfort anymore. It feels completely out of proportion, and I can’t talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any patterns or triggers that make your fear of flying worse, such as specific times of the year or certain types of flights?\n\nPatient: It’s less about a time of year, I think, and more about anything that suddenly makes the flight feel real. Like even if I only get an email about a rural shift that would require flying, my body reacts immediately.\n\nI’ll be sitting there doing something normal, and then I see the message, and right away my heart starts pounding. My hands get sweaty, and I get this feeling like I can’t breathe properly until I close the email. It’s embarrassing because nothing has even happened yet. I haven’t booked anything, I’m not at an airport, I’m not on a plane — it’s just an email. But my body reacts like I’m already trapped in it.\n\nSo I don’t know that I’ve noticed a seasonal pattern or anything like that. The trigger is more the possibility of having to fly, especially when it’s connected to work and I feel like I’m going to have to decide or respond.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed this kind of intense fear in any other specific situations besides flying?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I’ve tried to think about that because I don’t want to make it sound more mysterious than it is, but it really seems centered on flying.\n\nThere are other things in life I dislike or avoid if I can, sure, but nothing hits me the way the idea of getting on a plane does. That’s why I came in, honestly. The thought of boarding an airplane now scares me so badly that even while part of me knows it doesn’t make sense, my body reacts like the plane is going to fall. It feels like my body believes something terrible is guaranteed to happen, even when my mind is trying to argue against it.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t noticed that same intense fear in another specific situation. It’s the airplane part that seems to set it off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or fearful in situations other than flying, to the point where it significantly impacted your daily life or caused you distress?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. It doesn’t become this whole thing where I’m planning around it or feeling like I have to escape it. With flying, it’s more like the fear becomes bigger than the actual situation, and it starts affecting decisions before anything has even happened.\n\nSo outside of flying, no, I haven’t noticed anxiety or fear that significantly affects my daily life in that same way. It’s this specific situation, this fear of being on the airplane and believing it won’t stay up, even when I know rationally that sounds unreasonable.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time, since this fear started, where you didn't feel afraid of flying?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, since that flight, it’s been pretty continuous. There may be moments where I’m not thinking about flying because it’s not right in front of me, but if the idea of actually getting on a plane comes up, the fear is still there.\n\nIt’s been about seven months, since that turbulent small-plane flight after I lost my clinic job, and I can’t think of a stretch during that time where I felt normal about flying again. It hasn’t really let up in any meaningful way.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities or hobbies that you've had to change or avoid because of your fear of flying?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, not hobbies in the sense of, like, I stopped playing a sport or something, but day-to-day life has changed around anything that could involve flying.\n\nI’ve canceled clinic coverage because I knew I would have to fly for it, and I just couldn’t make myself do it. I’ve skipped medical meetings too, even ones I knew I should attend, because getting to the airport felt impossible. And seeing my family in Honduras has been put off again and again, which is probably the part that makes me feel the worst.\n\nEven going to the airport itself is not something I can do normally anymore. If I absolutely have to be there, I’m shaking, and I feel like I need to hold on to someone the whole time just to get through it. So I arrange my life to avoid getting to that point. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I know how it sounds, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other fears or anxieties that are as intense as your fear of flying?\n\nPatient: No, not like that. I mean, I worry about things, like anyone does, but nothing hits me the way flying does now.\n\nThat’s really why I came here. The thought of getting on an airplane scares me so badly that even as I’m telling myself, “This doesn’t make sense, planes fly every day,” my body doesn’t believe it. My body reacts like it knows for certain that the plane is going to fall.\n\nIt’s not that I have that same level of fear with other situations. This is the one that feels out of proportion, like I can understand it logically but I can’t make myself feel safe.\n\nDoctor: Are there any aspects of your daily routine or personal relationships that you feel are being significantly impacted by your fear of flying?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. It’s not just an inconvenience anymore. It’s affecting work and family in ways I’m ashamed of.\n\nFor work, I’ve turned down coverage opportunities because they involved flying, and that has cost me income. But honestly, the money is only part of it. I worry that my colleagues are starting to see me as unreliable, like they can’t count on me when something requires travel. I’ve also missed professional meetings that would have mattered for my career, and I know people notice when you keep finding reasons not to go.\n\nAnd personally, it’s been hard because I’ve delayed seeing my family. I keep telling myself I’ll figure it out or that I’ll go later, but later keeps getting pushed back. That makes me feel guilty, because this fear is making decisions for me. It’s interfering with my life in a way I don’t feel able to explain very well to people without sounding like I’m making excuses.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar fear when thinking about traveling by other means, such as boats, trains, or buses?\n\nPatient: No, not in the same way. I mean, I don’t love every form of travel, but boats or trains or buses don’t make my body react like that.\n\nWith flying, it’s different. Just thinking about getting on an airplane now scares me so badly that even though I know, logically, it doesn’t make sense, my body is telling me the plane is going to fall. It’s like I can argue with myself in my head, but it doesn’t change the fear. That’s really why I came in, because it’s not just a preference or discomfort anymore. It feels completely out of proportion, and I can’t talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any patterns or triggers that make your fear of flying worse, such as specific times of the year or certain types of flights?\n\nPatient: It’s less about a time of year, I think, and more about anything that suddenly makes the flight feel real. Like even if I only get an email about a rural shift that would require flying, my body reacts immediately.\n\nI’ll be sitting there doing something normal, and then I see the message, and right away my heart starts pounding. My hands get sweaty, and I get this feeling like I can’t breathe properly until I close the email. It’s embarrassing because nothing has even happened yet. I haven’t booked anything, I’m not at an airport, I’m not on a plane — it’s just an email. But my body reacts like I’m already trapped in it.\n\nSo I don’t know that I’ve noticed a seasonal pattern or anything like that. The trigger is more the possibility of having to fly, especially when it’s connected to work and I feel like I’m going to have to decide or respond.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed this kind of intense fear in any other specific situations besides flying?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I’ve tried to think about that because I don’t want to make it sound more mysterious than it is, but it really seems centered on flying.\n\nThere are other things in life I dislike or avoid if I can, sure, but nothing hits me the way the idea of getting on a plane does. That’s why I came in, honestly. The thought of boarding an airplane now scares me so badly that even while part of me knows it doesn’t make sense, my body reacts like the plane is going to fall. It feels like my body believes something terrible is guaranteed to happen, even when my mind is trying to argue against it.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t noticed that same intense fear in another specific situation. It’s the airplane part that seems to set it off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt anxious or fearful in situations other than flying, to the point where it significantly impacted your daily life or caused you distress?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I get stressed like anyone does, especially with work and responsibilities and all that, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had another fear that takes over my life the way this does.\n\nLike, there are things I might not enjoy, or situations where I feel tense, but I can usually still do what I need to do. It doesn’t become this whole thing where I’m planning around it or feeling like I have to escape it. With flying, it’s more like the fear becomes bigger than the actual situation, and it starts affecting decisions before anything has even happened.\n\nSo outside of flying, no, I haven’t noticed anxiety or fear that significantly affects my daily life in that same way. At least not that I can think of."
  }
